#6998. Inside Danielle Lauder’s Barbie Dream Closet

Designed by AMMOR Architecture, the ultra-pink, mega dressing room is a cinematic fantasy come to life.

Cher Horowitz had a touch-screen conveyer belt in her color-coded closet. Carrie Bradshaw kept her Manolos in an unusually spacious high-fashion-filled walk-in. And Margot Robbie’s Barbie displayed her hot-pink wardrobe like doll’s packaging in her wall-free fantasy villa. Danielle Lauder, the great-granddaughter of Estée Lauder, created her own iteration of the dream closet—this time, in real life.

Her own inspiration? “I always thought of Belle in Beauty and the Beast in the library, just rolling around on her ladder,” she says. “I’m not much of a reader, but as a collector, I knew that’s what I wanted.”

Her wish list for the jewel-box space also included curving walls, glossy pink lacquer, illuminated marble, and a capsule-shaped vanity inspired by the makeup collection she designed for Estée Lauder in 2019. The beauty empire was founded by—and named after—her great-grandmother in 1946; and her father, William P. Lauder, serves as the chairman of the board. (Danielle now oversees her own production company, Act 4 Artists.)

To make her dream a reality, Lauder turned to architects Goil Amornvivat and Thomas Morbitzer, founders of New York City–based AMMOR Architecture. The duo had instant chemistry with Lauder, who was especially taken with the meticulous diorama they created for her.

“Tom and I both love Barbie,” says Amornvivat. “So we tweaked our design process to make an architectural model that was more like a dollhouse, where you could switch out surfaces.” Their efforts worked on Lauder, herself a Barbie fan, and the pair walked away with the commission to not just create the dressing room but to overhaul the entire industrial-style apartment in the West Village of Manhattan, which was then being used as an art studio.

“They both really understood and saw my vision,” says Lauder. “Plus, they were the most excited, which made me more excited. Goil is like the Energizer Bunny.”

The 250-square-foot dressing room is, Lauder says, “the biggest room in the apartment besides the living room.” It’s also where Lauder spends the most time. She enters just off the primary bedroom via an arched passageway finished in custom bronze plaster, emerging from the dark portal into a pink fantasia where her expansive fashion collection is arrayed like a personal boutique. “The idea was for it to be part display area but also a functional closet,” she says.

At the center is a custom pink vanity outfitted with fluted drawers, dual-sided mirrors, and a top made of richly veined Midnight Rose marble, sourced from New York Stone. Lauder keeps her collection of vintage and reissued Estée Lauder compacts in the drawers. “I love collecting them and having them out on display or in a nice drawer,” says Lauder. “They’re beautiful little artifacts.”

On the curving walls, rounded niches display shelving and racks against illuminated stone, part of Lauder’s request for layered lighting. “I love the backlit marble,” she says. “It’s so flattering.” She keeps her enviable bag and shoe collection on permanent display, maintained with help from her housekeeper. Lauder draws from the selection on a daily basis, with particular affection for family heirlooms such as her mom’s vintage Dolce & Gabbana handbags.

Overhead, Amornvivat and Morbitzer devised a futuristic lighting installation, stretching mesh taut over an oval-shaped light source to create a seamless surface. “The light in there is controllable by color, and it's dimmable,” notes Morbitzer. “It’s kind of like a skylight, which was important because it’s the only room without windows,” adds Amornvivat.

The bespoke elements continue: heated floors, an ice maker, a perfume bar, plenty of drawers, and a pair of walk-in closets cleverly concealed behind matching lacquered doors. “The whole room is custom millwork,” says Amornvivat.

And Lauder did get the sliding ladder of her dreams. “That was my hill to die on,” she says. “I’ve always struggled to reach things in my closet, so it was definitely practical. And my grandfather had one in his apartment in the library when I was growing up, and I just loved it.”

Now that the closet is complete, Lauder has a hard time leaving. “I spend more time in here than I do probably even sleeping in bed,” she says with a laugh. The space is remarkably faithful to the original idea that Amornvivat and Morbitzer presented in their dollhouse diorama. “I wanted it to be totally mine, feminine and fun, and still very warm and inviting,” she says. “It’s become my ultimate bachelorette hub.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6991. AI Native Right Hand of the CEO - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

You dream of a new challenge, of being able to make a company scale and feel that you have a real impact.

If you dream of a life that is more like an adventure than a stable life, then becoming the AI-native right hand of our CEO is for you.

This isn’t another chief of staff or right hand job.

Most companies call themselves AI-first because they gave everyone a ChatGPT licence. We think that’s level 2, and there are 10.

At Paradox our ambition is $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents. Here, AI doesn’t assist the work. It runs the loops, in every department. Your job is to make that true for the whole company, side by side with the CEO.

Deep inside you are looking for a new adventure with…

🗽Freedom: working remotely or hybrid, and being trusted to deliver

🔥Ambition: building one of the first truly AI-native companies, with teammates who push AI to its limits every week

😍Impact: changing the trajectory of a whole company, not one line of a roadmap, with a product that transforms people’s lives at scale

Then, you have found the next adventure that will fulfill you.

In a company of fewer than 10 people, the right hand used to be the person who absorbed everything the CEO couldn’t do. Here, the right hand builds the machine that does it.

Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, decisions, procedures, member stories and past experiments, in one shared company brain that every person and every agent reads before acting.

Turn every decision into execution. A meeting, a voice note or a call with the CEO becomes briefs, tickets, follow-ups and deadlines, without anyone retyping it.

Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: agents read and write in our whole stack, from code to CRM, billing, analytics, ads, email, calendar and documents.

Ship. Produce the analysis, the contract draft, the page, the offer or the process, and put it live.

Measure with real KPIs. Revenue, cash, margin, retention and the transformation of our members, not activity.

Learn and loop on its own. Keep what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

People set the ambition, the taste and the standards. The machine runs the loop, and gets better every week.

This is already real. Our company brain holds our decisions and procedures. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment with its result. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars and audit our tracking after every live. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day, by text and by voice.

We believe self-help is broken. Most people use learning platforms the same way: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

They deserve more than content and more than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges and grows with them.

Human Intelligence (coaches, peers and mentors)

Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

into one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS, the Operating System for Human Greatness, augmented with AI.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world, and for something that will outlive any individual, its founder included.

Here’s the bet: A small team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

Reporting directly to the founder and CEO, you will help him run and grow the company.

Your scope is as wide as a CEO/COO’s: strategy, operations, finance, legal, people, product marketing and launches.

The difference is how you do it. In a classic company, the right hand coordinates people and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls. With fewer than 10 people, there is no department to hand things to. You build it.

❌The old way: a chief of staff takes notes in meetings, chases people by email and builds the weekly report by hand.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every CEO decision into execution. Meetings, voice notes and calls become briefs, tickets and follow-ups. Every Monday, the CEO gets one brief: what moved, what’s stuck, and what needs his call.

You decide what the CEO must see and what he never needs to see again. His time goes to vision, product and members, not to coordination.

Depending on your zone of genius, these are examples of strategic projects you could own:

Growth: run experiments with agents that generate, ship and measure them

Partnerships & sponsorships: find and close partners aligned with our brand, for our YouTube channel and events

International: build the machine that turns our French content into English, and open new countries

Launches: take a new offer or product from idea to live

People: find and assess the rare people who join a team of fewer than 10

❌The old way: each new initiative needs a team, a roadmap and three months.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: one person with the right agents launches an offer, a channel or a country without disrupting the core. That’s what our Pirate Squad means now.

❌The old way: an ops manager processes invoices, chases contracts, onboards people and answers the same support questions every week.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: finance, legal, HR, admin and customer support each get their agents, their context, their KPIs and their loop. You write the skills, plug them into our tools and write the evals that define "good": a reconciliation that matches to the cent, a contract that respects our playbook, an answer a member is happy with.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. An agent moves up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month, you build the agent that reconciles payments, refunds and failed charges every night. In month one it only reports. By month three it fixes the simple cases alone, and you only see the exceptions.

❌The old way: someone builds dashboards in a BI tool, exports them to a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody fully trusts the numbers.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: agents watch revenue, cash, acquisition, retention, churn and competitors continuously, find where our sources disagree, and explain what changed and why.

Strategy: screen markets and competition, build growth models, explore business models, partnerships and acquisitions. Then challenge, formalize and ensure good execution of the strategy.

At Paradox things move fast, and our CEO is always reading business books and meeting great entrepreneurs. You’ll turn the best strategies and tactics into systems and agents the whole company uses.

❌The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: every decision, result and lesson goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before acting. The company never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: the share of work done without a human, the time from decision to execution, the agents that moved up an autonomy level this month, and revenue per person. You push those numbers up.

You help the team become AI-native too: you spot the work still done by hand, and you build the agent or show a teammate how to build it.

And always: our members trust us with their lives. No fake claims, no fake scarcity, data handled with care. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. People do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. The company’s output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

Top tier Business or Engineering School (HEC, ESSEC, INSEAD, EM Lyon, EDHEC, ESCP, Stanford, …) or other high-level academic training (Les Mines, X, Sciences Po Paris, etc.)

A wide-scope role behind you: founder, chief of staff, founder’s associate, strategy, consulting or operations in a fast-growing startup

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, agent SDKs, MCP, automations, APIs, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Driven with strong work ethic: this position requires a strong commitment and perseverance

Entrepreneurship spirit: able to own a project from scratch, with few resources and little guidance, doing a lot yourself

Builder, not coordinator: you’d rather build the agent than chase a person twice

Taste: you know the difference between an output that works and one that is great, and you hold agents to the second

Growth over ego: you are at ease with regular feedback, obsessed with learning and growing, able to put your ego aside

Analytical and system thinker: you quickly identify and formulate problems, and solve complex ones with data

Excels in communication: both written and verbal

French: native or bilingual. English: fluent, you can hold a detailed conversation with English-speaking colleagues. Our chosen language for written communication is English.

We are inspired by a culture of performance and ambition. Everything is fast-paced and there is a lot of change. You won’t always be told what to do or how to do it.

We tell each other things, even if they are uncomfortable. We value growth over ego. At Paradox, expressing disagreement to a manager and knowing how to debate is valued! We encourage critical and strategic thinking.

❌ If you use AI for first drafts but your way of working looks the same as two years ago: wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage a lot of people: we aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each managing a dozen agents.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

If all this makes sense with you, then we’ll love working with you (and you with us, you’ll be pampered!)

Build one of the first truly AI-native companies from inside the CEO’s office, and write the playbook others will copy. You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

🏅Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform, whilst being in a good mood

🏋️‍♀️No more bosses, but ongoing conversations to support your growth

⚡️Learning to build with AI faster than almost anywhere else

🦄Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products are used by millions of people

🤩Waking up in the morning feeling like you are making a difference is something! We receive messages every day from members who quit smoking, reached a milestone in their business, built better relationships and changed their lives.

But that’s not all, we believe that driven people deserve…

😎A company culture based on autonomy & accountability

🗽Flexible working hours (but very hard working)

🌎A remote culture from day one with the freedom to work from wherever you want

🧍‍♀️Standing desks paid by the company if you live far from our offices

🏀350€ per year paid by the company to take care of yourself (for your gym, a dance class or whatever makes you feel great)

📚150€ of books or education: at Paradox, we are learner lovers

🔥Access to Athena, our AI coach, supporting you on anything personal or professional

❌ Any copy-pasted and non-personalized application goes directly to the trash can.

⚠️ To complete the recruitment process, each candidate must organize calls with his or her previous managers. If you are not comfortable with this: do not apply ⚠️

📃 A 6 to 8 minute application survey, with a short video and a link to something you built with AI

💻 A 30-minute video call with Timy, HR, to deep dive into your career path and ambitions

🛠️ A live build session: you solve a real Paradox problem with AI in front of us. We look at how you prompt, push back on the output and iterate, more than at a polished result.

💻 A 1h culture-fit video call, to assess alignment with our values and mindset

💻 A 1h final video call with David, CEO, on the company’s next milestones and how you’d build them

💼 Reference calls with your previous managers

If all this sounds good to you, come and write the next chapter with us 🚀🚀

#6989. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6987. More students are homeless as districts face enrollment and budget losses

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A report from SchoolHouse Connection shows 1 in 32 students were identified as homeless, the highest share on record.

Between the 2018-19 and 2024-25 school years, the portion of students experiencing homelessness increased from 2.7% to 3.1%, with the overall population of students experiencing homelessness increasing 12%. During that same period, overall public school enrollment fell by about 1.3 million students.

Some of the largest increases in the share of homeless students were seen in states where homeless student identification increased alongside drops in overall public school enrollment. Those states include West Virginia, Montana, Arkansas, Hawaii, Maine and Vermont.

West Virginia had the largest share of homeless students compared to its overall public school population at 1 in 16.

Districts in that state have also closed around 140 since 2011, according to the West Virginia Center on Budget and Policy. Thirteen school closures have been proposed or approved in just the 2026-27 school year, according to the center's last update in mid-October.

Overall, the state has lost 3.5% of its students since last school year and 17% over the past decade, according to an October count by the West Virginia Department of Education.

"The drop in enrollment raises two concerns for students experiencing homelessness: tighter school budgets, and children who may not appear in school records at all," said the SchoolHouse Connection report about the issue nationally.

The budget squeeze, drop in overall enrollment, and increase in students identified as homeless come on the heels of federal pandemic relief funds drawing to an end in September 2024. The American Rescue Plan's Homeless Children and Youth funds provided a historic $800 million to identify and serve students experiencing homelessness. The additional funding allowed over half of U.S. school districts to access Education for Homeless Children and Youth funds.

In 2025-26, the first full year without ARP-HCY funds, many schools no longer had the staff and resources that helped them find these students, said SchoolHouse Connection. The report noted that the lost funding is a potential reason most states identified fewer homeless students despite a national increase in that population.

In fiscal year 2026, Congress appropriated only $129 million for the EHCY program, which means about 1 in 5 districts receive dedicated funding for homeless students. The EHCY program was established under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act in 1987.

However, regardless of whether these funds are provided to districts, all districts are required under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act to serve homeless students and remove barriers to their education.

#6978. Is This the Ultimate Aspen Anti-Chalet?

Designer Rafael de Cárdenas, known for creating arresting spaces for Skims, Nike, and a host of New York City restaurants, worked with longtime client Rachel Dorrell on a mountain retreat with otherworldly glam.

Above: The primary bathroom of a home in Aspen, Colorado, designed by Rafael de Cárdenas with architecture by SAOTA. Custom tub with Dornbracht fittings; ceramic vase by artist Kazunori Hamana; side table by Rafael Prieto.

“We’re having a crisis of taste,” architect and designer Rafael de Cárdenas is in the midst of saying. “There’s a sameness. It’s all a historical mishmash.” He’s at breakfast in Manhattan drinking a cinnamon latte (the restaurant did not have the turmeric he requested). He has already completed his run in Central Park and his gratitude list in his Day One journal app, and he’s on day 735 following a visit to the Hoffman Institute—the famously mysterious weeklong “personal growth” retreat. “I feel like we’re in The Handmaid’s Tale,” he says. “AI is happening, but we’re going to pretend we’re designing manor houses in the English countryside in the 19th century?” Later he adds, “I have never been interested in recreating the past.”

So when longtime client Rachel Dorrell asked him to create a mountain house in Aspen for her growing family, no one expected a typical ski chalet. From her first visit to the plot of land, she realized the views of the town and the slopes beyond would be unmatched. Today those “crazy views” are framed by windows so large it’s hard to tell where rooms end and the outside begins. De Cárdenas, who collaborated with SAOTA architects to create the modernist structure, set about realizing Dorrell’s vision of “Colorado, but refined,” as she puts it. “Glamorous ’80s apartment” is how he describes the results.

“The mood board was Angelo Donghia,” that great of American design who worked with the likes of Liza Minnelli. “And Beetlejuice, always Beetlejuice! And Mickey Rourke’s apartment in 91/2 Weeks.” A cheetah-print rug sits in the library, while sculpted white custom sofas anchor the immense yet minimalist living room. “So many people have asked, ‘Shouldn’t there be art above the fireplace?’ ” de Cárdenas says. “I’m like, ‘Why?’ ”

Following the rules has never been his style. It has been 20 years since he launched his design firm, after studying at UCLA and assisting architect Greg Lynn at Vienna’s Angewandte institute and MAK museum, then returning to his native New York. The son of an erstwhile fashion PR executive and an actuary, de Cárdenas aspired to work for David Rockwell or for Alex de Betak’s fashion production company, where he once applied for a job. In the end he did a stint at Calvin Klein and worked as a production designer for films and commercials. Then a friend asked for help redoing a kitchen, something he did “kind of at night,” de Cárdenas remembers. Next came his first real client: actress Parker Posey. It was a revelation. “She was the first person I ever had to make window treatments for. I was like, ‘What the f***?’ I remember looking at a window and being like, ‘What is this supposed to look like?’ I didn’t understand that residential interior design is, essentially, fabric.”

Around the same time, he landed his first major corporate client, Nike, and did the design of aNYthing, a Lower East Side boutique that became a nexus for skaters, artists, and musicians in the late 2000s. “No one was doing stores like that at the time,” says creative director Jen Brill, who worked with de Cárdenas on several Nike projects. “There was nothing minimal about it. It was loud, it was fun. It was the first store I saw that was like retail theater.”

Another friend, Felix Burrichter, a curator and the founder of Pin-Up architecture magazine, recalls de Cárdenas’s early fascination with the Memphis Group architects (“when it was still considered bad taste,” Burrichter says) as well as his willingness to experiment with materials, such as applying duct tape patterns to the walls of a Jeffrey Deitch exhibition in Rome, or wrapping an Ikea chair with fluorescent gaffer tape. “For the aNYthing store, I physically built a lot of those things, because there was no one to do it and no budget,” de Cárdenas says. He sometimes laments not being so hands-on anymore, now that he has a team of 18 based in New York and Paris. “There was a lot of energy in those projects.”

“His work is like Limelight–meets–Park Avenue,” says Burrichter, who calls de Cárdenas the heir to ELLE Decor A-List designer Peter Marino, a maestro who works with significant private clients alongside luxury behemoths such as Dior and Chanel. De Cárdenas may have moved from duct tape to gold leaf applied by European artisans, “but there is always that tension in Raf’s work,” Burrichter says, “the binary between decorating and being an architect, between uptown and the club.”

Dorrell, for her part, became aware of de Cárdenas a decade ago, when she and her husband purchased a home in the West Village that he had staged. “We were like, ‘Who did the interiors and who did the art? It’s perfect.’ ” She says they were drawn to the ’70s vibe and the peachy hues, an unusual shade at the time. “We didn’t change a thing.” Since then they have worked together on homes in Austin, New York, and currently their largest yet, in Palm Beach. Dorrell sends “rapid-fire” texts of inspiration ranging from tiled walls at Lake Como’s Villa d’Este to barstools in Paris. “Raf cracks me up—we are brutally honest with each other,” she says, explaining that her taste skews more traditional, while his is often edgier.

“Some of my projects have moments that are ugly, and they excite me,” he says. “Because something truly excellent, something that is pushing boundaries, is not going to land pleasurably at first.” He points to Madonna, a frequent touchstone and source of inspiration for him. “The first time you saw Blond Ambition, the first time you saw Madonna with a high ponytail and with the headset, it was a little jarring. The thick eyebrow and the cone bra.” He often finds himself thinking, Where is that moment now?

Despite his concerns about the flattening effect of the algorithm, he’s still happy he’s in the business of taste. “I can’t believe I was lucky enough to land here, because I didn’t know what interior design even was,” he says. “I like my job. I’m grateful for my job. I want people to say, ‘He’s good at his job.’ ”

The Dorrells might concur: “ ‘We’re not doing another project,’ ” Rachel recalls her husband saying about Aspen. “Now he’s like, ‘Thank god,’ because it’s so awesome.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6977. Software Engineer - Interfaces Backend at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join Supabase’s Interfaces group and help run the backend services behind our developer tools, starting with postgres-meta: the open-source service that runs SQL against customers’ Postgres databases on behalf of our dashboard, our MCP server, AI agents, and other platform tools. You’ll work closely with the engineers who build those tools, and with our Platform and SRE teams.

AI agents are the fastest-growing source of postgres-meta’s traffic, and it sits on the path between developers and their data, so when it struggles, people can’t reach their databases from the dashboard or their agents. The team’s first priority is making it reliable, observable, and fair between tenants at agent scale, and helping redesign it for that scale without breaking the tools that depend on it. Depending on your experience, you’ll own major parts of that work or lead it. Alongside it, the team builds the backend services that give every branch of a project the configuration and secrets it needs.

We want to be upfront: this role carries real operational load. Our on-call process and rotation are new and need a lot of maturing, and you’ll help build them into something sustainable from your first weeks. You’ll be on call for services on the critical path, and incidents come before planned work. Platform teams run the infrastructure underneath; you’ll own the services themselves, from their code to how they behave in production. It’s ideal for someone who enjoys that kind of ownership, thrives in async, fast-paced environments, and stays calm when the root cause turns out to be in someone else’s system.

Help mature the team’s on-call from your first weeks: grow the rotation to cover every service the team runs, write runbooks, and agree when to declare an incident. Then build it, with our embedded SRE, into a sustainable follow-the-sun rotation as the team grows.

Set SLOs with the teams that call the service, and build the observability to hold them: metrics per tenant and per caller, alerts that catch failures wherever they start, and logs you can search across regions.

Make deploys routine with canary and staged rollouts, so dependency and security upgrades stop being risky.

Redesign how the service works with Postgres at scale: reusing connections, limiting or streaming large results, and isolating and rate-limiting tenants, so no single caller, human or agent, can degrade it for everyone else.

Help decide the service’s long-term architecture with our Platform teams, then evolve it without breaking the callers that depend on it.

Write down and agree the service’s contract with its callers, including the dashboard, MCP and AI, and platform workers: how transactions, multi-statement queries, timeouts, and errors behave.

Work through incidents with the networking, Kubernetes, pooler, and control-plane teams when a problem on the path to customers’ databases starts in their systems.

Build backend services for code-first workflows, starting with delivering each branch’s configuration and secrets to the services that need them.

Make small changes directly in the CLI, dashboard, or docs when a capability needs them, with the owning team reviewing.

Review code and designs, mentor other engineers, and grow into leading the team’s technical direction.

Have 5+ years of experience as a software engineer, most of it building and running backend services in production.

Have run a high-traffic, multi-tenant service in production: been on call for it, worked incidents through to resolution, and followed through on postmortems.

Write strong TypeScript, and work confidently in a large Node.js backend codebase.

Understand Postgres from the client side: connection management and pooling, transactions, statement timeouts and cancellation, and large result sets.

Have built observability for a service you owned (metrics, tracing, logs, and alerts tied to SLOs), and used it to debug problems that span proxies, load balancers, or Kubernetes.

Have kept a service healthy under load with patterns such as rate limiting, timeouts, memory limits, and graceful degradation.

Have shipped changes to a live service safely, with canaries, staged rollouts, feature flags, or fast rollback.

Work calmly and constructively with other teams during incidents, including when the root cause is in a system you don’t own.

Communicate clearly in writing, from design docs to postmortems, thrive in async, globally distributed teams, and make progress when the path isn’t fully defined.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have rebuilt or migrated a live, high-traffic service without breaking its callers, using techniques such as shadow traffic, contract tests, or incremental replacement.

Have led the design of reliability work, such as tenant isolation, load shedding, or a staged-deploy pipeline, or set technical direction that other engineers built on.

Have built or operated a database proxy or connection pooler (such as PgBouncer, Supavisor, or pgcat), or an API gateway such as Envoy.

Have secured a service that runs SQL on its callers’ behalf: handling credentials safely, and limiting how much damage any one caller can do.

Production experience in a second backend language, such as Elixir or Go.

Experience with a secrets manager or key management service, such as HashiCorp Vault or AWS KMS.

Familiarity with Supabase, or with how AI agents use databases through tools like MCP.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#6967. Jackie Kennedy’s Private Letters to Truman Capote Reveal a Fuller Picture of Their Friendship

Heading to auction on November 21, the six notes are estimated to fetch as much as $80,000.

First Lady Jacqueline Kennedy’s sister, Lee Radziwill, was one of celebrated author Truman Capote’s “Swans,” developing a close, if complicated, friendship with the novelist. But Jackie had her own relationship with Capote, one that is less widely known. The pair corresponded with each other in the 1960s and Kennedy may have even gotten an early look at Capote’s 1966 novel, In Cold Blood.

“I have your precious book and am so overjoyed,” she wrote to Capote in an undated card, presumed to be from June 1963. “The minute Jack takes off for Europe I will retreat to the country + indulge myself by reading every page—not just the new ones.”

That letter, along with five others between Kennedy and Capote, will soon hit the auction block, ELLE Decor can reveal. The notes, three of which are written on White House letterhead, will be sold together in a single lot through Cornell Auctions, estimated to fetch between $40,000 and $80,000. Together, they reveal new details about the friendship between the former first lady and one of America’s great authors.

Available for the first time ever, these private letters are part of a larger auction of Capote-linked items accumulated over decades by his biographer, Gerald Clarke, author of 1988’s Capote. The sale, “The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote,” will take place on November 21.

Kennedy’s notes are all believed to have been written in 1963 and 1964, with four bearing postmark dates. During that period, Kennedy suffered personal tragedies, losing her infant son Patrick days after he was born as well as her husband, President John F. Kennedy, who was assassinated in November 1963.

The letters indicate a warm relationship between Kennedy and Capote. In several of the messages, the first lady makes reference to various presents and mementos she received from the writer.

One particularly meaningful gift came in the wake of President John F. Kennedy’s death, after Jackie and Capote saw each other at a gathering hosted by decorator Billy Baldwin. “The most beautiful porcelain rose that I ever saw or dreamed of—came to me the other day from you,” the former first lady wrote in a three-page December 1964 missive, handwritten on mourning stationery bearing a crest.

In another letter, Kennedy makes reference to a gift she sent to Capote, inspired by a visit to the novelist’s home. “In your Japanese-Victorian room I spotted two large apothecary jars of the same kind—I found this one in 1955 in Paris—so it has been with me for many years in many places,” she wrote in an undated letter, believed to be from December 1964.

Handwritten on black-bordered stationery, that note was addressed to Capote’s house at 70 Willow Street in Brooklyn Heights, where he wrote Breakfast at Tiffany’s and In Cold Blood.

Read Jackie Kennedy’s Letters to Truman Capote

Beyond the Kennedy notes, also up for auction is Capote’s four-piece formal dinner invitation from President Lyndon B. Johnson in 1966, which has an estimate of $600 to $2,000. Other highlights include a German pencil sharpener in a leather pouch; a chalkware bulldog; a monogrammed duffel bag; and jewel-like paperweights made by the author himself.

Taken together, these pieces paint a fuller picture of Capote’s life and work as a writer, as well as Kennedy’s enthusiastic support of his talents. “I keep thinking what power a great writer has. All the things you write move people,” she wrote. “I am glad you became a writer.”

“The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote” will take place November 21 starting at 11 a.m.; bidding can be done online via LiveAuctioneers or in person at Cornell Auctions in Bellport, New York.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6966. Today’s classroom isn’t broken–but it wasn’t built for today’s top students

#6965. Increases in pre-K discipline rates alarm advocates

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Preschool enrollment grew just 12% between 2021-22 and 2023-24, but suspensions increased 67%, an analysis by two equity groups found.

The number of U.S. preschoolers receiving out-of-school suspensions increased 67% and the expulsion rate nearly doubled between the 2021-22 and 2023-24 school years, according to an analysis of federal data by EdTrust and AdvancED Equity.

The rise in discipline rates for the youngest students may, in part, be due to a growing preschool enrollment, said Maithreyi Gopalan, co-author of the report and co-lead of AdvancEd Equity. Enrollment in school district-led preschool increased from 1.4 million in 2021-22 to 1.5 million in 2023-24.

Enrollment could be inching up due to a rebound from sharp drops during the COVID-19 pandemic, as well as expansion of public preschool programs in recent years, said Gopalan, who is also an associate professor at the University of Oregon's College of Education.

But the increase in students disciplined outpaced the overall enrollment growth of preschoolers, Gopalan said. "It's kind of shocking, isn't it, when you're thinking about 3- to 5-year-olds. What are they getting suspended for or expelled from school?"

The analysis by EdTrust and AdvancED Equity of preschool discipline rates uses data from the U.S. Department of Education's Civil Rights Data Collection and does not include information about what types of incidents led to the discipline.

A recent report from The Children’s Equity Project at Arizona State University found that harsh punishments used in preschool programs can lead to negative, long-term impacts like lower academic achievement and reduced high school graduation rates.

The CRDC only includes statistics from school district-supported preschool programs, Gopalan said. That means preschool enrollment would be included for district-operated early childhood Head Start programs, but not for Head Start programs run by community grantees.

And while the CRDC data is critical for tracking national trends, it does not capture the whole picture of discipline activities throughout the early childhood education system, said Gopalan, adding she and other researchers are continuing to look at different data collections to "get a better sense of what's going on."

What the CRDC data does reveal is that, like K-12 exclusionary discipline data, disparities in preschool punishments exist. The 2023-24 CRDC data shows Black preschoolers made up 18% of the pre-K population but were 37% of students who received suspensions. Boys were 54% of preschool enrollment but accounted for 81% of those suspended. White preschoolers were also overrepresented with 44% of the pre-K population but 51% of those suspended or expelled, according to the EdTrust and AdvancED Equity report and Gopalan.

Gopalan also noted in a follow-up email that the CRDC is limited, as it only collects formal out-of-school suspensions and expulsions. She said there is no federal collection that tracks more informal preschool discipline activities like shortened days, repeated pick-up calls or when parents are counseled to withdraw their child from a preschool.

Social-emotional learning interventions can help prevent escalating and challenging student behaviors, Gopalan said. And teacher training and resources also can reduce educators' stress and increase their capacity to address difficult behaviors.

"I think we as a society need to ask, 'Do we want to suspend them, or do we want to provide additional supports for teachers to help manage this problem?'" Gopalan said.

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

#6955. This London Townhouse Was Designed for the Holidays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Miles Redd and David Kaihoi created the ultimate English Christmas retreat for longtime clients—cozy, festive, and swathed in reds and greens.

Above: In the living room, the seating is by Luther Quintana with Samuel & Sons fringe. The wallcovering is by Iksel, and the cocktail table has a Fornasetti top.

Some homes are for summers by the water, others for weekends on the slopes. When Krystyna Houser set about redecorating her family’s London townhouse, she was thinking of Christmas. “We spend every Christmas there,” she says. At this house, that meant more than just putting up a tree.

Located near Richmond Park, in southwestern London, the 19th-century townhouse has been in Houser’s family for more than 20 years. It was always a place to return to while Houser, founder of the nutritional startup mBIOTA Elemental, and her husband Dan, a co-founder of Rockstar Games, were raising their three children in New York City. Dan had grown up nearby, and the ample greenspace that gives the area its name made it an appealing place to visit with a young family. The interiors, however, had never quite received their due.

Houser turned to Miles Redd and David Kaihoi, the partners behind the New York–based firm Redd Kaihoi. She had known Redd since 2012, when she hired him to decorate a Brooklyn Heights townhouse, and the two became friends. They have since worked on multiple houses together. For London she trusted the designers to craft the warm and celebratory atmosphere she craved. “I wanted it to be equally elegant, cozy, and family-oriented,” she says.

Red and green were a natural starting point, as green already had the family’s allegiance. “It’s one of their favorite colors,” Redd says. “So we leaned into that a lot.” Verdant hues appear in velvet, felt, and marble throughout, while the entrance hall is dressed in crimson. These were holiday colors with no intention of coming down in January, and Redd was going for sophistication rather than holiday kitsch. “I wanted romantic 19th-century elegance, the feeling that they’re stepping into a Merchant Ivory movie.”

This approach suits a firm known for exuberant color and layered furnishings. Redd brings a sense of theater to traditional decorating, drawing on the work of such legendary designers as Billy Baldwin, Albert Hadley, and John Fowler and mixing lacquer, chintz, and bold geometric patterns. Before opening his practice in 1998, he trained with antiques dealer John Rosselli and decorator Bunny Williams. Kaihoi, a sculptor, joined the ELLE Decor A-List firm in 2008 and became a partner in 2019. Together the duo give grand rooms an ease and wit that keep formality in check.

Houser considers “interior designer” an insufficient description for Redd and Kaihoi. “I feel like they are set curators,” she says. She gave them considerable freedom, occasionally inviting them to surprise her with potential purchases. “I don’t think it’s my place to hinder creative expression, and, as a result, the rooms are just magical.”

The house’s existing architecture gave that freedom some useful structure. “It had great English bones,” Redd says. He retained the original living room mantelpiece, library doors, and front door, while upstairs he and Kaihoi worked to restore character lost to earlier alterations that had stripped away 19th-century details. An excavation created additional basement space for a screening room and a ping-pong area, giving the family more places to gather and play.

From first visit to completion, the project took the better part of an unhurried decade, with pauses for permits, the Covid pandemic, and other commitments along the way. All the while Redd and Kaihoi kept shopping at auctions, antiques stores, and fairs, which ensured that the rooms would have an accumulated character. The designers looked to the guards at Buckingham Palace for the entrance hall and main staircase, borrowing the red and gold of their uniforms for its matka silk walls trimmed with gold braiding. Beyond the hall, the green velvet–lined library opens into a living room papered to resemble a coromandel screen.

Upstairs, the primary bedroom and a sitting area occupy a long, narrow space. Green felt screen dividers give the bed its own area in one corner without a wall breaking up the suite. “It’s all sort of one big bedsit, which is a very English notion,” Redd says. A small painting of a parrot on one of the screens keeps Houser company at her bedside.

The couple wanted their suite to feel like a luxurious hotel—inviting places to read and work, a comfortable bed, and a generously scaled bathroom—without sacrificing a sense of home. “You’ve ruined hotels for us,” Redd recalls Houser telling him of their previous work.

Step Inside the Ultimate Christmas Retreat

But Houser’s favorite rooms are the ones that bring everyone together: the kitchen and the adjoining striped family room, which open onto the garden. It’s here where everyone gathers for meals and to relax. The connected spaces provide separate places to eat, play games, and watch television, allowing the family to spend time together even when everyone is doing something different.

“Their design helps bring us together,” Houser says. “And that’s what it is. It’s a family home.”

#6948. Bulgari Brings High Jewelry to a Palm Beach Stronghold

To celebrate its new boutique, the Italian house unveiled its Eclettica collection at a quintessential South Florida estate.

Above: To celebrate Bulgari’s new collection and boutique, guests roamed the grounds of a waterfront Palm Beach estate, designed by architect Addison Mizner.

Few people shaped the look of South Florida as much as architect Addison Mizner. A pioneer of Spanish Colonial Revival architecture, his Mediterranean style quickly took Palm Beach by storm in the 1920s, as he designed and built more than 50 Florida mansions and villas. And his impact still lingers, as, on Wednesday night, stars like Venus Williams and Chanel Iman spent their evening seated under the tennis pavilion at one of Mizner’s famed waterfront Palm Beach estates.

The crowd was there to celebrate a far newer building in the neighborhood: Bulgari’s Palm Beach boutique, which opened earlier this week. In honor of the store’s debut, the brand brought its newest high jewelry collection, Eclettica, to the United States for the first time.

“Palm Beach embodies a distinctive spirit of effortless sophistication,” said Bulgari CEO Laura Burdese.

Located in a heritage building in Palm Beach’s shopping district, the boutique is modeled after a traditional Italian piazza. Anchored by four travertine columns, the open space encourages clients to move through the maison’s most famous collections. Venini’s classic Serpenti chandelier, made of Murano glass and seen in Bvlgari boutiques worldwide, serves as a focal point in a shape reminiscent of the brand’s many symbolic snakes.

Elsewhere in the store, decorative panels by Milan-based Pictalab depict Floridian ocean and beach views, grounding the Italian maison in its surrounding landscape. Among the art is the Eclettica collection, which borrows its name from eclecticism and stays true to its moniker: mixing inspirations from painting, sculpture, and architecture, translating them into bold, colorful pieces.

It’s a fitting style for Palm Beach, where Mizner’s vibrant Mediterranean and Spanish-influenced designs borrow inspiration from entire eras and regions, giving the area its everlasting mix-master flair.

“We are delighted to bring Eclettica to this extraordinary destination,” Burdese said, “where its bold artistic vision and exceptional craftsmanship find a natural resonance with the city’s vibrant character.”

#6944. Upstart: Principal Engineer, LLM

At Upstart, we’re united by a mission that matters: to radically reduce the cost and complexity of borrowing for all Americans. Every day, we bring creativity, experimentation, and advanced AI to reshape access to credit, helping millions move forward financially with clarity and confidence.

As the leading AI lending marketplace, we partner with banks and credit unions to expand access to affordable credit through technology that’s both radically intelligent and deeply human. Our platform runs over one million predictions per borrower using more than 3,000 signals, powering smarter, fairer decisions for millions of customers. But the numbers only hint at the impact. Every idea, every voice, and every contribution moves us closer to a world where credit never stands between people and their financial progress.

We’re proudly digital-first, giving most Upstarters the flexibility to do their best work from wherever they thrive, alongside teammates across 80+ cities in the US and Canada. Digital-first doesn’t mean distant. We’re intentional about in-person connection through team onsites, planning sessions, and moments that spark creativity and trust. And whether you choose to work primarily from home or collaborate in-person from one of our offices in Columbus, Austin, the Bay Area, or New York City, you’ll have the support to work in the way that works best for you.

If you’re energized by tackling meaningful problems, excited to innovate with purpose, and motivated by work that truly matters, we’d love to hear from you.

At Upstart, our Applied AI team's mission is threefold:

Agent Platform: Build and support the platform that makes it straightforward for non technical users across Upstart to build, launch, and operate AI agents (voice/chat/text) reliably.

Borrower and Applicant Assistant Orchestration: Build the orchestration layer behind a unified personal assistant that shares context across specialized agents, giving applicants and borrowers a cohesive, assistant-like experience.

Borrower-Connected Internal Workflows: Improve internal operational workflows that directly connect to applicant and borrower experiences, making them faster, smarter, and more efficient.

As Principal Engineer, LLM you will serve as a technical pathfinder and force multiplier for this team and the product squads we partner with. Rather than owning internal developer-tooling infrastructure or training foundation models from scratch, your mandate is to compress the time between an ambiguous idea and a reliable, user-facing AI system, while raising the applied AI bar across every squad you touch.

This role operates on an explicit 80/20 charter:

0-to-1 Product and Architecture Pathfinder (~80%): Rapidly prototype high-ambiguity bets across our agent platform, cross-agent context orchestration, borrower-facing assistant experiences, and internal workflows. Partner directly with Product and Design to validate non-deterministic UX, establish clean architectural patterns, and hand off battle-tested evaluation contracts for engineering squads to scale.

AI-Native Engineering Catalyst (~20%): While dedicated internal teams own Upstart's broader AI developer tooling and software-factory initiatives, you will serve as a visible practitioner on the product side. You will lead by example with agentic coding workflows and tight verification harnesses, partner with our internal tooling teams as an early adopter ("customer zero"), and help product squads adopt higher-leverage ways of working.

Because AI systems are non-deterministic, user experience is part of the architecture. Whether you are designing cross-agent handoffs, borrower-facing assistant flows, or internal operational tools, you will navigate real-world trade-offs across latency, unit economics, reliability, and user trust, and push back constructively when a simpler deterministic approach is the right call.

De-risk 0-to-1 bets across the stack: Rapidly prototype ambiguous initiatives spanning multi-agent context sharing, UX for non technical users, platform primitives for launching agents, and borrower-connected internal workflows.

Deliver durable evaluation contracts, not throwaway handoffs: Treat prototype code as disposable proof of concept, and hand off to engineers what they actually need to build production services with confidence: golden datasets, automated evaluation harnesses, documented failure-mode maps, latency/cost benchmarks, and clean architectural blueprints.

Partner cleanly from spike to production: Align early with receiving squad tech leads to surface system constraints, support joint go/no-go decisions with Engineering and Product leadership (including killing unviable bets early), and stay engaged through initial productionization to review early PRs and debug edge-case model behavior.

Design non-deterministic UX and orchestration together: Treat latency spikes, context loss between agents, subtle hallucinations, and fallback paths as systems design problems, shaping experiences that earn and protect borrower and operator trust.

Make pragmatic applied AI trade-offs: Default to the simplest reliable solution first (prompting, context engineering, deterministic guardrails), and make clear architectural calls on model routing, fine-tuning, or distillation when latency, unit economics, or reliability require it.

Raise the applied AI bar across squads: Model disciplined agentic coding and verification practices in your daily workflow, feed practical requirements back to internal tooling teams, and elevate engineering judgment across teams through active collaboration.

Software engineering foundation and live AI shipping: 8+ years of software engineering experience, along with hands-on experience shipping non-deterministic, user-facing LLM or AI products to real users.

Agentic and LLM systems architecture: Deep practical experience designing production LLM systems involving structured outputs, tool calling, context engineering, retrieval/memory patterns, and multi-step or multi-agent orchestration (including state management and context sharing across agents).

Production systems, latency, and reliability engineering: Strong distributed-systems and backend fundamentals for non-deterministic pipelines: async orchestration, streaming UX, caching, timeouts and fallback paths, rate-limit handling, and end-to-end observability/tracing to diagnose failures.

Evaluation and quality engineering: Proven experience building golden datasets, automated evaluation harnesses (deterministic checks, heuristics, and LLM-as-a-judge), regression gates, and systematic failure-mode analyses.

Safety, guardrails, and applied AI pragmatism: Strong instinct to reach for the simplest reliable tool first (deterministic logic, prompting, context engineering, guardrails against hallucinations and prompt/data risks), paired with sound judgment on when latency, unit cost, or reliability justify routing, fine-tuning, or distillation over frontier APIs.

Full-stack prototyping fluency: Comfortable moving fast across backend and frontend stacks (such as Python, TypeScript/React, and modern REST or streaming APIs) to put working end-to-end prototypes in front of users and stakeholders quickly.

Low-ego pathfinder collaboration and UX empathy: Track record aligning early with partner squads, handing off ownership cleanly without ego, and partnering directly with Product, Design, and operations or business leaders to turn ambiguous goals into concrete latency budgets and resilient fallback experiences.

Bachelor's degree in Computer Science, Engineering, Mathematics, or equivalent practical experience.

Startup or 0-to-1 incubation DNA: Experience operating in early-stage or low-boundary environments where you owned ambiguous problems end to end under real constraints.

Agent platform and shared abstractions: Experience designing reusable agent primitives, SDKs, or orchestration platforms that help peer engineering teams build, evaluate, and launch AI workflows safely and quickly.

Production fine-tuning and distillation reps: Hands-on experience fine-tuning or distilling smaller models in production to improve latency, unit economics, or task reliability.

AI-native engineering habits: Deep daily fluency with agentic coding workflows and automated verification harnesses, plus experience helping peer engineers adopt higher-leverage development practices.

Experience in regulated or high-trust consumer domains (such as fintech, lending, or healthcare) where accuracy, explainability, PII protection, and user trust are critical.

#6939. Coaching key to Alabama’s literacy success, report finds

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The state’s approach to placing a dedicated literacy coach in every school influenced the success seen later in Mississippi, a Fordham report says.

“The story is so fascinating to me because it’s very rare in education that you can draw a straight line from policy to achievement scores improvement. And in this case, people across the state seem to agree on what works—because they spent the early 2000s doing it," said Loewus.

Beginning in 2012, Alabama, however, reduced funding while expanding the coaching cadre to K-12 and across other subjects like math. By 2019, Alabama ranked last in the nation in 4th grade reading.

In the meantime, Mississippi had noticed Alabama’s early success and replicated the model. “They flip-flopped,” Loewus explained. And by 2019, Alabama looked at Mississippi and passed comprehensive legislation that more or less reinstituted its reading initiative, with funding for 750 school-based coaches, 100 regional coaches, and 10 staff at the state Department of Education to monitor the entire process, she said.

The Alabama legislation also protects coaches’ time: they cannot be pulled into substitute teaching when someone calls in sick, proctor tests, or help with recess. “That is so unusual,” Loewus said. “In other schools, coaches are the first ones to be pulled. They don’t have their own class.”

The state superintendent Eric Mackey requires district superintendents to sign a memorandum of understanding before they receive their annual allocation, and “when principals start to pull coaches for other things, he gets a phone call,” she added.

Overall, the legislation in Mississippi is mostly similar, Loewus said. The program there was initially funded by a now-shuttered philanthropic group called the Barksdale Reading Institute, but the state has since taken over the budget. “It’s really top down in both places,” she said. “It’s key having really resolute state leadership, champions at the top who are not letting this go.”

Loewus emphasizes that the coaching alone won’t achieve desired results unless the coaches are centrally trained in research-based reading instruction.

“The fact that everyone is getting the same training, and it’s centralized, and based on research, is really important. Protecting coaches’ time is really important. Having steady funding is important," Loewus added. "Having the structure of coaches having coaches is incredibly important.”

About 44 states and Washington, D.C., have passed science of reading policies, according to a 2025 update from Whiteboard Advisors.

#6936. On the Couch: Tina Barney

In honor of her new book, the celebrated photographer shares some select sources of inspiration.

In 1974, Tina Barney moved from New York City to Sun Valley, Idaho, a quiet, picturesque ski hamlet set within the Rocky Mountains. It was there, while taking an art school class on great American photographers, that she began shooting photographs herself. In the five-plus decades that followed, Barney became a legendary American photographer in her own right, with works entering the permanent collections of museums like the Whitney, the Museum of Modern Art, and the Los Angeles County Museum of Art.

The daughter of 1940s fashion model Lillian Fox and granddaughter of the founder of Lehman Brothers, Barney is known for documenting high society with a sharp eye, photographing affluent Americans, European aristocrats, and lavish spaces. She also captured what they wore—and shot for fashion magazines and major designers like Gucci and Bottega Veneta. Barney’s relationship with style, stemming from her mother’s work with fashion photographers like Horst P. Horst and George Hoyningen-Huene, is the subject of her new book Tina Barney: Fashion, out now.

In honor of her book’s release, Barney took our On the Couch survey, sharing a few of her biggest influences and inspirations.

Greatest Influences: I made a film on Jan Groover. I made a film on Horst P. Horst. And you can see the photos he took of my mom in the new book.

Favorite Sofa: My couch has been in this house for decades, and I am probably getting a new one because this one's falling apart. But it is in the right place with the right light. I can watch TV; I can read; I can draw; I can do anything I want from this place. And it's comfortable.

Most beloved buildings: I did a whole book on the Noguchi Museum, a portrait of Isamu Noguchi’s building, in a way, and of his sculpture.

Destination Museum: The Musée d’Orsay. It’s the way the paintings are hung that really interests me. There are artworks placed within little cubicles, and then, of course, there are the most well-known paintings that are just so easy to keep looking at. So the Musée d'Orsay first, followed by almost any other museum in Paris.

Go-to galleries: It depends on the artist that is showing. In New York, I go out to look at galleries every month with a friend. We’ll see what the new shows are, and I always try to learn from what I see.

Favorite Artist: I don't like to name names because there are no favorites. But I just saw a Brice Marden show at Gagosian that was tops.

Favorite getaway: I travel as little as possible right now. I live in Rhode Island part-time, more so than in New York. Here it is quiet.

Best Shop for Gifts: Well, I love agnès b. She’s very supportive of photographers, so that’s how I came to know her, and I still visit her shop in New York.

Style Icons: I don't think of buzzwords like that. But, my mother for sure. Her style was led by her sense of humor, and by her own personality. In those days, you weren't thinking about the fashion designers and who was in and who was out. And so the clothes had much more character to them.

My mother brought me to an Yves Saint Laurent fashion show in Paris when I was about 16, which I remember very well. It was the first show that Yves Saint Laurent ever had on his own. We also saw the designer Guy Laroche; we went in after the show and my mother had a suit made for her, so they measured her. And that was a pretty fantastic experience.

Fashion Designers: I have a picture of me from about when I was age 14 and I'm wearing a suit that looks like a Chanel. And because they were starting to copy her at that point, you could get a viable suit for someone 14 or 16 years old that looked like a Chanel. I love that the style of Chanel has existed for so long, and has not changed that much.

Required Reading: Katie Kitamura. Also, Judith Freeman, my favorite author, who I know is working on a new novel.

Cocktail of Choice: I don’t drink; I never have. I’m like a monk.

Favorite Restaurant: The Olympia Tea Room in Watch Hill, Rhode Island.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Inside Beatty and Bening’s Former Mansion

This London Townhouse Was Made for Christmas

Danielle Lauder’s Dreamy West Village Closet

Inside Taylor and Travis’s Cleveland Suburb

#6926. A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Isabel López-Quesada honored the historical essence of a 19th-century Menorcan retreat while introducing vibrant colors and an elevated ease.

Above: In the living room, López-Quesada replicated the original ceiling beams and finished the walls in traditional Menorcan lime plaster. A sofa of her design is grouped with a 1960s Tito Agnoli rattan daybed, a Mexican palm leaf stool, and a custom cocktail table upholstered with a Moroccan rug.

For Spanish designer Isabel López-Quesada, summers tend to revolve around Biarritz, in southwestern France, where she has a home. Still, she always carves out time for Menorca, regarded as the most laid-back of Spain’s Balearic Islands. That vibe suits the ELLE Decor A-list designer, whose signature style is luxurious but unfussy, harmonizing old and new with breezy refinement.

Typically, López-Quesada goes there to relax and spend time with friends, but around five years ago she received a call from a Bilbao family who asked her to refresh their vacation home outside Mahón, Menorca’s capital. An old farm property, or finca, the five-bedroom residence required extensive updates. López-Quesada was steadfast in preserving such historical elements as a well and donkey stables, which she transformed into an indoor-outdoor bathroom with a patio and marble tub. “It was important to respect the style of Menorca,” she says. “We were very careful with the materials, so it all goes together and conveys the sentiment I want the house to have.”

López-Quesada founded her namesake studio as a precocious 20-year-old Madrileña 40 years ago. Initially inspired by her mother’s yen for decorating and the work of celebrated Spanish designers such as Paco Muñoz and Fernando Benjumea, López-Quesada garnered wide attention in the early 2000s, when she converted an old Madrid wax factory into an atelier and a luxurious home for her family, filled with antiques and contemporary pieces. Soon she had interest from clients all over the world. Today López-Quesada’s 12-person team includes one of her three adult children, daughter Isabel Llanza. Five years ago the pair co-founded the lifestyle brand Isita Home, selling products they make themselves or discover on their travels.

The Menorca project began by gutting the house’s interior and rebuilding it “in an old style,” López-Quesada says. Central to it all was adding custom windows and doors to each room so the island’s golden light could flood the spaces. Vaulted and beamed ceilings were left intact or were recreated. Reconfiguring the floor plan, López-Quesada eschewed a proper dining room in favor of an eat-in open kitchen. “Most of the time you are eating outside,” she says.

With Menorca’s unrelenting summer sun, bougainvillea-draped pergolas were a must for leisurely alfresco lunches and dinners. Swaths of white lantana and lily of the Nile border the terraces, while “the pool reflects the sky like a mirror and provides a refreshing feel,” López-Quesada says.

Her collaborator on the gardens was Spanish landscape architect Luis González-Camino. “My vision was to design a water-efficient Mediterranean garden, divided into distinct yet connected ‘garden rooms’ for different seasons and times of day,” he says. González-Camino preserved the property as a natural Mediterranean woodland, with wild olive trees providing cover.

Indoors, López-Quesada installed French terra-cotta floor tiles chosen for their aged look. The three fireplaces were all handcrafted in traditional Menorcan style—a little imperfect, painted white—and decorated with vintage tiles the designer keeps in her Madrid warehouse.

She offset the white ceilings and lime plaster walls with vibrant textiles and tilework in blues and greens inspired by Menorca’s native palette. An upstairs bedroom is anchored by an iron four-poster bed with a blue-and-white-striped canopy; its bath has a shower lined with lapis-colored zellige tiles. Doors, which across Menorca are customarily painted in bold shades, here are finished in verdant hues.

Custom cabinets are designed with whimsically patterned cutouts for airflow, since humidity is a major factor. “On the islands, if you close the cupboards completely, mushrooms will grow inside,” López-Quesada says.

Step Inside This Renovated Menorca Retreat

She arrayed the spaces with a lively mix of vintage and new furnishings, emphasizing organic materials: rattan chairs, bamboo shades, Moroccan rugs, lots of linen and cotton fabrics in spirited florals or stripes. “If I use a heavy piece, then the rest is lighter,” she says. The tableware maintains the blue and green theme, including pieces from Isita Home and hand-blown glassware from neighboring Mallorca.

In the end, the owners asked López-Quesada to redo their home in Spain’s Basque region as well. It’s one of several projects she currently has across the country, including reimagining a hotel in Barcelona, and she’s also designing homes in Paris, Shanghai, and the Dominican Republic. Many of her projects are with repeat clients. “That’s how it works,” the designer says. “It’s like a chain.”

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6918. How to build an ‘attitude of gratitude’ across grade levels

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Helping students build skills around gratitude can have benefits ranging from daily mindset to overall classroom culture, a UC Berkeley professor says.

Building skills and habits around finding and expressing gratitude can improve students’ day-to-day mindset, their longer-term emotional and mental health, their interpersonal interactions and overall classroom culture.

Educators can help bring this about, although they need to be schooled in how to do so at each developmental stage, according to Vicki Zakrzewski, education director at the Greater Good Science Center at the University of California, Berkeley.

Gratitude is a relational virtue that requires recognizing that someone has done something for you, and that there was a cost to what that person did, Zakrzewski said.

“Gratitude communicates that you really matter,” she said. “Students have to have that ability to recognize that somebody else has done something for you. Gratitude can be fairly complicated when you break it down, cognitively and emotionally.”

For the youngest students, that means teaching what gratitude is in the first place by reflecting on what the emotions surrounding gratefulness feel like, Zakrzewski said.

“You’re helping them define it,” she said. “When is a time somebody did something kind for you? How did that feel? … Students don’t really get a sense of pure gratitude until they have the ability to empathize with others. You’ll see it become more relevant to students who are around 2nd or 3rd grade.”

By middle school, students have the ability to think larger, beyond their own circles, about people who have made a difference in society. When addressing people they know personally, Zakrzewski suggests having them write a gratitude letter.

“That’s one of the most powerful activities,” she said. “If they can, have them deliver it to the person and read it out loud.”

However, educators might have to take a different tack with high school students, who can tend to roll their eyes at a gratitude-related activity. Zakrzewski suggests starting by involving them in a conversation about what gratitude is and how to express it, and then launch an activity so it becomes part of their daily life.

“Of course, there’s the traditional gratitude journal, every day or once a week, where you say what you’re grateful for,” she said. “A powerful thing with high school students in particular is to do self-science: have them notice how they feel before and after they do a gratitude journal."

Students could then decide how often they want to keep a journal, and keep notes about how they feel in week one compared to week four.

Research has shown that when somebody does something for you, it enhances your self-worth and improves relationships, Zakrzewski said. And improved relationships are important in building classroom culture.

Another activity Zakrzewski recommends is transforming complaints into gratitude.

"If you find your students are starting to go down the complaining route, and you want to help change that mindset, take the complaint, but turn it into something you feel grateful for,” she said.

For example, if there’s a math test on Friday, a student might reflect that they are grateful they get to exercise their brain and how that can help them in the future. Cultivating the habit of gratitude and seeing the world through that lens removes a feeling of entitlement, which research has shown provides mental, emotional and physical health benefits for adults, Zakrzewski said.

“That’s a beautiful way of going through the world,” she said. “If you can cultivate that as a student, the benefits are tremendous.”

Research also has shown that not all cultures practice gratitude in the same exact way, and educators should delve into that aspect, as well, Zakrzewski said.

“That’s really important for teachers, if they’re going to do gratitude activities,” she said. “Have conversations with students’ parents, or students themselves, about how gratitude shows up in your culture.”
