#6914. The Design Fiend’s Guide to Tangier

The Moroccan city, a complex, culturally kaleidoscopic crossroads between Europe and Africa, is home to a savvy community of creatives whose stylish impact continues to grow. Word is out.

Above: The pool terrace at Villa Mabrouka, a 12-room hotel that was once the home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé, with gardens designed by Madison Cox.

Writing about Tangier in the 1950s, Truman Capote observed that “the days slide by less noticed than foam in a waterfall.” Decades later, this city on Morocco’s coast, across the Strait of Gibraltar from Spain, is significantly changed from Capote’s time, when it was an exotic retreat for café society’s demimonde, people such as Barbara Hutton and Malcolm Forbes. In recent years Tangier’s mosaic of villages—from the medina’s warren of whitewashed lanes to the Ville Moderne’s blocks of Art Deco towers to the hillside villas and gardens on Jebel Kebir—has been reinvigorated, in part by an enterprising mix of Tanjawis (the locals) and expats who continue to infuse the city with a fresh creative spirit.

Yet there is much that remains the same: the dreamy light that seduced artists from Eugène Delacroix to Henri Matisse to Mariano Fortuny, the overgrown gardens of casbah courtyards, and the cooling breezes coming off the confluence of Atlantic and Mediterranean waters. And there’s still plenty of the languorous lifestyle that captivated Capote.

“Tangier is all about settling in,” says Charlotte Claydon, whose whitewashed Moroccan Art Deco house has been in her family for more than a century. Known as Dar Sinclair, the residence sits in a grove of papyrus and lilies between thick palms and weeping willows. It’s one of numerous historic villas and townhouses renovated by international designers and other creatives who live here that can be rented. There’s arguably no better way to ease into the rhythms of daily life in Tangier. Pin Affleck, who arranges travel and accommodations through the Tangier Bureau, says that “going into a private house, you get the sense of being inside Tangier.”

When Claydon and her sister Jacquetta Wheeler began renting out Dar Sinclair, they received messages from guests worried about filling up a week. “We spent hours coming up with lists of activities, knowing full well they might only get to one or two,” Claydon says, noting that most guests make their own discoveries and end up on unplanned diversions.

Among the sisters’ can’t-miss activities: tea at Café de Paris, Sunday service at historic St. Andrew’s Church, and a camel ride, especially with kids, at Achakar Beach. A typical morning in Tangier might begin with breakfast under a banyan tree at El Morocco Club. Around 11 a.m. the markets buzz to life, including the legendary fish market in the Grand Socco, the Fondouk Chejra (weavers’ market), the antiques mall on Rue de Hollande, and the Casabarata flea market, where a sharp eye is required to spot the gems amid the seedy furniture and cheap fabrics.

Lunch in town at Alma Kitchen or El Dorado is a must for crispy falafel and plates of lamb chops. Later, as the sun sinks into the sea, it’s hard to beat the views from the roof of Villa Mabrouka, the onetime home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé that Jasper Conran transformed into hotel with gardens by Tangier resident Madison Cox. After a martini, the hotel’s L’Orangerie restaurant is a smart spot for a dinner of thistle tagine and grilled sole; down the street, the atmospheric Hamadi serves standout harissa soup and sizzling skewers.

Claydon and Wheeler, for their part, also advise guests to reserve time for relaxing and being transported at home, one of the great pleasures of staying in a design-centric private residence. Other places available to rent include the red-and-white-striped riad owned by decorator Gavin Houghton and photographer Boz Gagovski (who took most of the photos in this story), as well as antiques dealer Gordon Watson’s large ochre house. Both weave together Moorish and European details, with objects from Tangier and around the world that reflect the city’s cosmopolitan history. As Hamish Bowles noted in his foreword to the book Inside Tangier, “The magic of this place was that everyone who had chosen to make a home here brought their own fantasy with them.”

That book was produced by my husband, designer Nicolò Castellini Baldissera, and our home here, a 1929 townhouse named Casa Tosca, is filled with a worldly mix of objects and furnishings that we designed. Outside, our terraced garden was overseen by Umberto Pasti, whose own Tangier residence is a collection of 1930s bungalows set within meandering gardens of datura trees, rambling roses, and alocasia. For us, so much of the pleasure of this place is being at home and having people over. Creating the setting with local craftspeople and market finds becomes a pastime in itself.

It’s the same for Marco Scarani and Jamie Creel, whose house, Dar Zero, has had an illustrious lineage of owners who have contributed to its rich layers. The home is in the casbah, with a bushy fig tree and bougainvillea spilling from its courtyard over the front wall. Inside, iron klismos chairs from when designers Yves Vidal and Charles Sevigny lived there sit on well-worn bejmat and zellige tiles. There are also pieces of their own creation, such as brass bird-foot candlesticks they make in Tangier and sell in their New York shop, Creel & Gow. “There is a lot of transmission from one generation to another,” Scarani says. “Friends here inherit objects and even homes. We live in houses that have the spirits of the people who came before.”

For designers Frank de Biasi and Gene Meyer, it was the relationship with artisans that prompted them a few years ago to open Habibi Burton, their boutique where they offer pillows, painted tables, and a rotation of decorative objects, all sourced locally. “Making things for the shop gives me more freedom than making things for clients,” says de Biasi. “Because we aren’t designing to a deadline, I can have four artisans contribute to a single piece.” He also recommends Las Chicas for fashion, Kasbah Collective for regional crafts, and the bookshop Les Insolites.

Down the street, artist and curator Hicham Bouzid oversees Kiosk, a cultural salon and shop that hosts talks and exhibitions bringing together creatives from Africa, the Arab world, and Europe. It has a selection of books and artist prints, as well as a restaurant and live music. Bouzid is one of the native Tanjawis who are animating the city’s cultural life. Another is Yto Barrada, perhaps Morocco’s best-known contemporary artist, who founded the popular Cinéma Rif, where screenings of contemporary and classic films share the program with talks and other cultural events.

Across town Clos Nadir, the house shared by designer Jean-Louis Deniot and William Holloway, exists in a different world, perched among pines and looking out across the strait. Focusing on spaces for entertaining, Deniot placed a 30-foot dining table on a roof, while his living room is outfitted with a large fireplace, plush carpets, and a welcoming mix of furniture, mostly made locally. “Tangier is a land of artistic opportunity, a place for aesthetes, where stylistic adventures, contemplation, and the freedom to create are central to daily life,” says the ELLE Decor A-List designer, who is opening an office here.

Tangier’s close-knit community of creatives comes alive during the summer, which is when Veere Greeney, another ELLE Decor A-List designer, hosts an annual theatrical performance in the gardens of his mountaintop villa, Gazebo. Producer Rob Ashford assembles actors from Broadway and the West End (last summer it was Dames at Sea, featuring Darren Criss and Julianne Hough), and the shows are open to the public, though tickets are limited. It’s a full sensory experience colored by the scent of jasmine and gardenias, one that captures the eccentric artistic allure of Tangier.

The energy and interest in the city continue to grow. Deniot is creating a hotel with theater producer Jordan Roth, the Four Seasons recently announced a new property on Malabata Bay, and King Mohammed VI is behind a luxurious renovation of the historic Hotel Continental.

And while there is excitement around these new projects, what appeals most about Tangier is something older. Over lunch on Grenney’s terrace, looking out at Spain, we discussed the city’s past lives. “What makes Tangier unique is its varied history and strategic position,” he observed. “The strait made it a city of diplomacy, openness, and inclusiveness. That is its bedrock.”

#6908. Internal IT Manager at Clutch

The Internal IT Manager owns the Service Desk and the Corporate Systems that run the business, and builds the AI driven workflows that connect them. You run the team and you also build: you design the automations, ship the integrations, and show the team how it is done. You set priorities, coach each person, and build alongside them.

You lead a small, hands on team that keeps the company running and builds toward AI Native. The team shares one queue, reviews metrics, and brings automation ideas to the company.

ServiceDesk and ticketing• Run the ServiceDesk across onboarding, offboarding, access requests, hardware and software support.• Own the ticketing platform: queues, categories, SLAs, escalation paths, and reporting.• Cut ticket volume through self service, knowledge base content, and AI assisted triage and resolution.Asset management• Maintain one accurate inventory of hardware, software licenses, and SaaS subscriptions.• Manage the full asset lifecycle: procurement, provisioning, tracking, refresh, and secure disposal.• Reconcile licenses against real usage to remove waste before each renewal.Corporate systems• Administer the core business stack: identity and SSO (Okta), device management (Iru), collaboration tools, and the systems used by People, Sales, Legal, and Finance.• Keep systems secure, documented, and available, with clear owners and change control.• Evaluate new tools and retire those that no longer earn their cost.Corporate contracts and vendors• Manage IT vendor relationships, renewals, and negotiations.• Maintain a contract register with owners, terms, renewal dates, and spend.• Work with Legal and Finance on reviews, security questionnaires, and budget forecasts.

AI and enterprise workflowsYou treat AI as a core tool for running IT, and you build with it yourself.• Design and ship automated workflows for onboarding, offboarding, access provisioning, approvals, and renewals.• Integrate systems through APIs, webhooks, and iPaaS tools so data moves between ticketing, identity, HR, CRM, finance, and contract platforms without manual steps.• Deploy AI agents and assistants for ticket triage, knowledge search, request fulfillment, and employee support.• Write scripts and light code (Python, JavaScript, PowerShell) when a ready made tool falls short.• Set guardrails for AI use: data handling, access scope, review steps, and audit trails, agreed with Legal and Security.• Measure each automation by hours saved, errors avoided, and adoption, then improve or retire it.

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off and National Holidays: Enjoy 20 PTO days yearly and the National Holidays for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

Please note: This position is offered on a contractor basis. Applicants must have the necessary documentation and authorization to work in the country where the job is located. Clutch cannot provide sponsorship or assist with obtaining work permits for this role.A Note About AI at ClutchWe love AI. We use it often and encourage our team to creatively and effectively leverage AI tools in their work. If you join Clutch, we hope you'll bring the same enthusiasm for exploring how AI can amplify impact, productivity, and innovation.That said, during the interview process, we want to hear your thoughts. Please approach interviews without the use of AI tools—our goal is to get to know how you think, solve problems, and communicate. Once you're in the seat, bring on the prompts!

#6905. Are Zen Hotels Now Putting Us to Sleep?

Rich, layered maximalism is the latest form of escapism—historically inspired decor spiked with creative contemporary flair. Get ready to Instagram every nook.

Above: One of the cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, CA.

On the top floor of a Gilded Age McKim, Mead & White mansion in New York, now reincarnated as the Fifth Avenue Hotel, Martin Brudnizki recently put the finishing touches on a two-bedroom suite dubbed the Flâneur Penthouse. With his hallmark exuberance, the Elle Decor A-List designer arrayed the space with distinctive flourishes: a spirited Scalamandré wallcovering trimmed in grosgrain ribbon, bespoke furnishings mixed with French Art Deco pieces, India-inspired inlaid nightstands, Japanese-style lamps, and Murano glass ceiling lights. Each element connects to an imagined narrative that shaped Brudnizki’s vision for the hotel: “this cinematic idea of the home of a flâneur who has traveled the world, has seen and acquired a lot, and put it together here,” the designer says. “It’s a multicultural journey.”

Hotels in this vein tend to be -owner-operated properties looking to carve out a niche. One could trace a baroque family tree that links mold-breaking 1980s boutique hotels like Ian Schrager and Philippe Starck’s Royalton in New York to Jacques Garcia’s interiors for the Hôtel Costes and L’Hotel in Paris to Ilse Crawford’s Ett Hem in Stockholm to Kelly Wearstler’s Proper Hotel in Austin.

Fulk’s hospitality design, like Brudnizki’s, embraces spaces inspired by history and storytelling, with an emphasis on artistry, material richness, and character. Both men’s work is rooted in tradition, with idiosyncratic twists that feel fresh. And they have plenty of kindred spirits working in a diversity of styles, including such designers as Lorenzo Castillo, Kit Kemp, and Laura Gonzalez. While far from a coherent movement, the sumptuous, high-impact aesthetics practiced by these designers just might be today’s most zeitgeisty current.

Take Fulk’s Cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, California, each one an elevated boho mashup of influences. “Sixties and ’70s rock and roll, and a sort of Laurel Canyon meets Northern California spirit,” explains the designer. “The cottages feel utterly like nowhere else.” And that, of course, is the point.

For his part, Brudnizki doesn’t care much for maximalism as a label. In the case of his glamorously moody interiors for the Broadwick Soho in London—shimmering surfaces, lavishly upholstered furnishings, a riot of pattern—everything began with an idea he describes as “Studio 54 meets your grandmother’s Soho townhouse, a disco granny kind of thing.”

Then there are the roughly dozen memorable properties created by Kit and Tim Kemp under their Firmdale Hotels group. The most recent is the Warren Street Hotel in New York, with the forthcoming Bedford Place Hotel slated to open next year in London next to the British Museum. “It’s about personality, a sense of arrival, and fun,” says Kit Kemp, who outfits each of her hotels with a more-is-more mix of her ebullient textiles and wallpapers in splashy colors, animated by art and craft.

Kimberley Cohen, the creative director behind Maisons Pariente, says her family’s small portfolio of hôtels de charme are about creating a residential-feeling experience that is also “something you have never seen, that you would not do in your own home.” She has worked with Brudnizki on Le Grand Mazarin in Paris, with Charles Zana on two hotels in the South of France, and with Pierre Yovanovitch on a Méribel chalet. Her latest project, opening this winter, is a Courchevel retreat named Saint Roch, with interiors by Friedmann & Versace that channel the chic romanticism of traditional dachas.

Next up for Brudnizki, meanwhile, is the Knox Hotel, set to open soon in Dallas, new territory for him. As for what one should expect of the decor, he sums it up as “100 percent unapologetic glamour. It won’t be boring.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6901. People Business Partner, GTM at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

We're looking for a Senior People Business Partner for our GTM org. You don't need to be the deep expert in every corner, but you're dangerous enough across all of it to move fast and recognize when something deserves more attention than you can give it right now. You also know when to slow down and when to raise the harder question early.

You build the kind of relationships where leaders and individual contributors bring you the hard stuff while there's still time to proactively do something about it. You're approachable, direct, and willing to push back when something doesn't make sense. You don't need every answer, but you know how to ask good questions, work through messy situations, and help leaders make better decisions.

You'll partner with leaders across Sales, Marketing, and the rest of our revenue organization as they build and grow their teams, and you'll work closely with our VP of People and the broader People team to build scalable, intentional People programs that enable folks to do their best work.

You'll thrive as a People Business Partner if you:

Take the time to understand the business just as much as the people side

Have worked in enough different environments to have a real sense of what good looks like, and hold yourself to it

Stay steady when a sales org is moving fast: quota changes, reorgs, and rapid hiring don't rattle you

Are direct with people, including when it's uncomfortable—feedback doesn't get softened into confusion, and people still trust you after

Assess risk quickly—not just what could go wrong, but what's actually likely to and how much it matters

Know when to escalate and when to just decide; you bring a recommendation, not just a problem, and you know which things (comp exceptions, anything legal, changes to established policy) always get a second set of eyes

As a People Business Partner at LiveKit, you will:

Own performance management, manager coaching, org design, and headcount planning as the primary People partner for Sales, Marketing, and our broader Revenue organization, including pre- and post-sales teams

Navigate complex situations and help the org grow without breaking, including pushing back on adding layers or headcount before the case is made

Partner with our GTM Talent Partner on hiring plans, role scoping, and ramp so headcount decisions are made with the full picture

Work as one team with the other People Business Partners (EPD and G&A) so leaders get a consistent partnership experience

Work directly with the VP of People and larger People and Talent team to design, build, and operationalize People programs across all COEs

Own end-to-end delivery of programs and projects, from strategy to rollout to iteration

Make our work repeatable: documented, handoff-ready, and not dependent on anyone being a hero

Experience as a People Business Partner (or equivalent) supporting Sales and other revenue-generating teams: performance management against quota and ramp, comp plan changes, rev org reorgs, and coaching sales managers

Think from first principles and know when to ask for help

A clear, direct communicator who can challenge strong personalities while maintaining trust

Confident in what you know and what you don't—able to respond decisively even without full information

Comfortable in ambiguity, with strong relationship-building instincts and data-informed decision-making

Personable, approachable, and comfortable working with leaders and ICs at all levels

Understand how hiring, talent development, and organizational decisions impact a sales team's success

Experience with global HR, leaves of absence, or benefits administration

Direct experience building or significantly iterating programs across multiple People COEs

Experience using AI tools to improve how you work, with good judgment about when and how to use them

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Work alongside world-class people at one of the most important AI infrastructure companies being built right now

#6892. Senior Software Engineer II, Agentic Apps at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Creators use Kit's first-party apps and integrations to connect Kit to the rest of their business. Today we build and maintain these apps by hand, with help from an agency partner. That doesn't scale. We're looking for a Senior Software Engineer II to own app delivery and maintenance end to end, so a small team can support creators of every size.

You'll be the first engineer on the Apps squad. Your focus is shipping high quality apps at a high pace, and maintaining them to an industry-best standard. To get there, you'll take a systems-level view and build on Kit's coding agent. You'll work closely with our AI Developer Experience squad, which builds the coding agent. You'll own the creator experience of every app you build, and you're free to improve Kit's platform when that makes those apps better.

This is a full-time IC role for an AI-first, product-minded, full-stack engineer who has shipped agent workflows to production and judges them by reliable outcomes, not lines of code. You combine technical depth with leadership and extreme ownership. You take input from Partnerships, Product, and Design to help shape the "what," and you own the "how." You thrive in ambiguity, take initiative without waiting for permission, and elevate everyone around you through your generosity and optimism.

We're excited about first-party apps because they let creators do more with Kit than Kit does on its own. Our own apps should just work, and set the bar for every app built on Kit.

You'll report to John Bohannon, Senior Engineering Manager for the Ecosystem and Apps squads. You'll collaborate closely with a Partnerships Manager, Product Manager, Product Designer, and engineers on the Ecosystem squad. You'll also work closely with our AI Developer Experience squad on Kit's coding agent, and with a contractor who helps maintain our apps.

The Apps squad works as a sibling to Ecosystem. You'll set its rhythms, collaborate with our Partnerships Manager on which apps to build next, and join Ecosystem's cycle planning.

A peer onboarding buddy will help you learn our systems and codebase, and you'll have engineers across Kit for support and mentorship.

First Week: Set up your local environment and merge your first production code. Meet the team through Get To Know You's. Start the app handover with the contractor who maintains them today. Watch the recorded app walkthroughs and read the runbooks for our most-used apps.

First Month: Audit our first-party apps and integrations, starting with the apps that need the most maintenance. Measure what each app costs to maintain, including partner app reviews like those at Google, Meta, and Shopify. Learn how Kit's API, OAuth, and App Store support creators from small to large. Fix bugs and support escalations in the apps you now own. Learn how Kit's coding agent works with the AI Developer Experience squad.

First Three Months: Adapt Kit's coding agent to build your first apps and maintain existing ones. Give it the context, guardrails, and evals it needs. Turn what you learn into shared pieces every app can use, like a reusable load test, infrastructure automation, and app manifests. Write runbooks that let others work on any app. Drive each app's maintenance toward zero human time with the coding agent.

First Six Months: Ship new apps at a steady pace. Take over the rest of the app catalog and apply the coding agent to its maintenance. Show that one engineer with agents can maintain many apps at high quality. Define the metrics and dashboards for agent-built apps: launches, reliability, time to fix, and revenue impact. Share how agents build and maintain our apps, for example in a Lunch & Learn.

First Year: Kit ships more high-quality apps, faster, and the coding agent handles more of the routine builds and fixes, with you steering the system. The legacy integration model is retired. You use catalog metrics to recommend where the Apps squad invests next. You and the AI Developer Experience squad improve the coding agent together, so other Kit engineers benefit too.

Expert in agentic AI development. You've built agent workflows that ship and maintain production code, with evals, guardrails, and human review.

Strong in APIs, including OAuth, webhooks, and integrations with third-party platforms. Fluent in Ruby on Rails, or able to ramp into it fast. Full-stack, with React where an app needs it.

Ship well-tested, performant, accessible code, whether you or an agent writes it. Set the quality bar and build the tests, evals, and reviews that hold agents to it.

Set up dashboards, alerts, and load tests that find problems across many apps early.

Measure each app's maintenance cost, and make the team's open capacity for new apps easy to see.

Communicate clearly across Engineering, Product, Design, and Partnerships.

Align others on goals, trade-offs, and success criteria without formal authority.

Led a major cross-team initiative from ambiguous requirements to delivery, partnering with senior leaders across functions, with measurable business impact

Built agents or tools that measurably improved how a team ships software, not only how much code it writes

Took ownership of a codebase someone else built, with thin documentation, and made it maintainable

Independently managed a large project, such as a migration or deprecation that required customers or partners to move

Create leverage, not only features. Invest in reusable systems that make your team and others faster.

Judge AI work by outcomes, like launches and reliability, not by the volume of code or PRs. Share what works and what the risks are.

Own everything you and your agents produce, from code to PR descriptions. Know the details well enough to stand behind every line, and don't leave reviewers or readers to do the work.

Lead work without waiting for a formal role or permission.

Make reversible product decisions on your own, and know when to pull in cross-functional stakeholders like Product, Design, Partnerships, and Support.

Break big, ambiguous problems into steps that you and others can deliver.

Write docs that let others work on any app, so the work doesn't depend on one person.

Built AI tools that other developers use, such as MCP servers, agent skills, or eval suites

Shared your work through talks, blog posts, or open-source contributions

Wanting a large team of engineering peers on your own team from day one

Avoiding direct feedback, whether giving or receiving it

#6890. The 9 Best Umbrella Stands for Weathering Any Forecast

These holders will brighten up even the rainiest of days.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A rainy-day return often leaves a small mess by the front door: drops on the floor, nowhere for guests to place their rain gear, and damp umbrellas leaning against the wall. An umbrella stand gives them a proper spot to drip-dry and keeps them together for the next time the forecast changes.

Umbrella stand designs have as much variety as the entryways that host them. A terracotta holder brings earthy texture; powder-coated metal can take on a graphic shape; leather adds a softer touch. Even when the skies clear, painterly decoration and bold color can make the stand part of the room’s decor.

Whether you’re welcoming drenched guests or making space for one well-loved umbrella, the right holder keeps rain at the threshold.

Triangles of hand-bent birch support a brass tray that catches errant droplets.

Two curved metal sheets face each other in a modernist abstraction of an embrace.

Fornasetti’s classic illustrations are a wink at this receptacle’s contents.

Interwoven strips of vegetable-tanned leather play with negative space.

Panes of smoked glass and metal joinery are a sleek approach to everyday storage.

Made with hand-stitched calfskin leather, this option has a touch of the equestrian.

Guests will be glad to leave their brollies in the care of a regal crested crane.

This rustic Italian terra-cotta cylinder comes with a healthy dose of patina.

This hexagonal form is made vibrant with traditional Vietnamese lacquer.

#6887. Lady Gaga’s Former Manhattan Penthouse Hits the Market for Nearly $7 Million

The Hell’s Kitchen condo also has ties to Carmelo Anthony and Tracy Morgan.

Forget a “Million Reasons”—we’re looking at $7 million. The New York City penthouse where Lady Gaga once lived just hit the market for nearly $7 million—and she’s not the home’s only celebrity connection. The 3,700-square-foot duplex was also once home to former NBA star Carmelo Anthony and 30 Rock actor Tracy Morgan, per People.

Located in the Hit Factory Condominium, the unit is perched atop a residential complex that once housed the famous recording studio of the same name. The 1928 building on West 54th Street was the second home of the Hit Factory, where Grammy-winning artists like Michael Jackson, Madonna, U2, and Mariah Carey recorded songs.

In 2005, the music studio moved locations again, and the building was converted into loft-style luxury condos.

On the market for the first time since 2018, Gaga’s former unit comes with three bedrooms—two on the lower level and a primary suite upstairs. The top-floor space includes its own walk-in closet, bath, and wrap-around terrace that measures over 1,000 square feet and features sweeping city views.

The listing notes that the unit—once listed as a rental for $24,500 a month—has undergone a “gut renovation” and includes a double-height entry foyer and chef’s kitchen outfitted with appliances from Bosch, Sub-Zero, and Viking. Featuring white-oak flooring throughout, the residence also includes a home office, in-unit laundry, and an array of built-in storage solutions.

For her part, Gaga recently welcomed her first child with fiancé Michael Polansky. While the family is keeping a low profile, they are believed to reside primarily at the artist’s vast Malibu estate, which the native New Yorker acquired in 2014.

“They have an amazing biodome of love,” a source recently told Page Six. “Seems like a really safe place for her.”

421 West 54th Street, #PHC is listed for $6.995 million with Douglas Elliman.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6882. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6879. At the Frick Collection, Louis Vuitton Finds Itself Among the Masters

The French house’s sponsorship of the Frick includes a fashion show and a landmark exhibition of Italian bronzes.

Above: Louis Vuitton kicked off a three-year partnership with New York’s Frick Museum with its cruise show in May.

Bright lights go up, Peaches’ “Boys Wanna Be Her” starts pounding, and a girl wearing layers of denim begins strutting past such luminaries as Zendaya, Chloë Sevigny, and Ava DuVernay. In many ways a normal New York fashion event—except the rooms are adorned with Renaissance paintings, Qing Dynasty vases, and 19th-century gilded French furniture.

The exalted surroundings are in fact the galleries of the Frick Collection, the Gilded Age treasure trove that was recently reenvisioned with the help of Elle Decor A-List architect Annabelle Selldorf. The fashion, music, and glamour are courtesy of Louis Vuitton, which has made a tradition of working with cultural institutions and heritage sites. The French house kicked off a three-year sponsorship of the Frick with this show in May, and now the first exhibition with the company’s support is open: a landmark show of Italian bronze sculptural masterpieces.

The reliefs, figurines, medals, and candelabra were drawn from collections as wide-ranging as Dresden, London, Washington, and Siena, the focus of the show. Donatello’s tomb of the Bishop of Grosseto, a life-size traffic stopper of a slab, timeworn yet still lyrical, is worth a visit alone, as is the miniature, gilded zinc brass relief of Bellerophon and the Chimera by Francesco di Giorgio. In an era of endless swiping, the lesson of the Chimera—and of these timeless bronzes—endures: be wary of desire’s fast fulfillment. Instead, wander into the rooms and stay a while.

#6874. A Kitchen in Bloom: How Tiffany Howell and Miele Reimagine the Heart of the Home

Eschewing conventional design for an moody, romantic kitchen, this wildly glamorous space proves that high-tech convenience can foster intimacy, slowness, and the art of living well.

When Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Interiors designs a home, her vision transcends conventional norms, veering into an ethereal realm—an approach she calls “the poetry of place.” In Howell’s mind, film, fashion, and music are as essential as decorative art, and she uses these inspirations to create theatrical spaces that ignite all the senses—layering texture, sound, scent, light, and form to evoke deep emotion. When executed with this level of intention, a room becomes an environment you don't merely look at but fundamentally feel.

When Miele partnered with Howell to create a dream kitchen for an installation at Art Basel, she seized the opportunity to reimagine what a kitchen can be. The result is the interactive 360-degree rendering seen here—a wildly imaginative digital environment that inspired the brand’s upcoming physical installation at Miami’s premier art fair. “I wanted an undeniable sexiness, a beautiful, sensory experience,” she says. “I created the space with characters in mind that I’d invite to a dinner party in this kitchen.” After all, the kitchen is a home’s heartbeat, an interactive space with multiple purposes.

“I do think the kitchen is really one of the most tender rooms in the house,” Howell says. She leaned into that romance by installing black cabinetry with blossoming branches and birds in iridescent greens and pinks. The cabinets are an homage to her love of 1920s chinoiserie furniture and fashion, seen through a very glamorous, Los Angeles lens. That California focus also meant bringing the outdoors in. The grand conservatory space harnesses diffused, natural light with the promise of the scent of jasmine wafting into the room. Howell’s ideal playlist sets the tone with jazz greats and ambient synth-pop.

“I wanted to create a kitchen that’s a completely sensory experience,” Howell explains. “The sound of a crackling warm fireplace, the aroma of a meal cooked for loved ones, or of making coffee in the morning.” For Howell, these aren't incidental details; they are the sensory architecture that turns raw space into lived emotion. In Howell’s view, this kitchen is “also a place where you can take a nap, read a book, or write in your journal. Every moment in this room can become a ritualistic experience of living well.”

When it came to the practical needs of a kitchen, Howell used Miele as a muse, an invitation to eschew the pale minimalism so common in luxury kitchens. “The appliances are so elegant, they kind of act as the jewelry of the room,” she says. “And their functionality and ease of operation give you back time to live better, relax with a drink, hang out with family and friends, and create the stories that make up a life.”

Consider the Miele Built-In Coffee System and wine cooler in matching dark finishes which are nearly camouflaged so the technology reads more as furniture than appliance. That tension between heirloom design and high-tech styling becomes a unified part of a lived-in, well-collected home. Accent pieces, including a vintage Fortuny pendant lamp over the kitchen island, and upholstered chairs in the conservatory with detailing reminiscent of antique lingerie, create a moody environment well suited to Miele's black-finished lines that resemble rich obsidian.

The 36” Full Surface Induction Cooktop features a frameless, flush design that disappears into a dark-veined countertop. Transforming the entire glass surface into one expansive cooking area, it fits up to six pots or pans at once and with Intelligent Pan Recognition and SmartSelect White controls seamlessly transfer power settings wherever pans are moved. On the same wall is the 30” Handleless Combi-Steam Oven XXL, with DualSteam technology that emanates ASMR sounds of steam cooking, and a black glass façade that resembles a framed panel or window. From the cool, flush glide of the induction cooktop to the whisper-quiet hum of precision steam, the technology engages the senses rather than interrupting them, turning modern convenience into an intuitive, tactile ritual. Rather than breaking up the wall with a stack of conspicuous appliances, these seamless integrations keep the architecture artful. And for added convenience, Miele’s elegant touch controls are enhanced with Wi-Fi connectivity for an easier user experience.

Howell’s concealment of technology is exhibited in her use of the 42” French Door Refrigerator with its elongated custom handles and a dark lacquered paneled front that echoes the floral chinoiserie motifs—an unconventional solution that turns a mundane appliance into a piece of decorative art. Once the refrigerator doors are open, the Brilliant Light Pro illumination makes it easy to survey the food inside the large capacity interior.

“How do you nurture yourself and others in the kitchen?” Howell asks. Her answer: a design that creates a restorative space evoking intimacy and slowness, where technology can encourage a lightness and give us back more time. By composing every sight, surface, scent, and sound into a single harmonious narrative, Howell proves that a kitchen shouldn't just be an aesthetic statement—it should be something you feel in your bones. “What excites me is building a new world with more possibilities,” Howell says. “I don’t think I could design any other way.”

#6873. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

Research released around the recent Black Hat USA 2026 conference demonstrates that artificial intelligence is increasing the speed and scale of cybersecurity activity without replacing the attack methods defenders already face.

#6870. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

Above: Listed for $75 million, the Hillandale estate sits on 268 acres in Stamford, Connecticut.

Fortunately, three extraordinary Gilded Age homes are currently on the market, offering the chance to live out that fantasy in real life. Take a peek inside below.

Dating to the turn of the century, this lavish European-style estate—known as Hillandale—once belonged to the Sulzberger family, owners of The New York Times. Located along the Connecticut border with New York, the star of the 268-acre compound is an eight-bedroom mansion designed by architecture firm Hunt & Hunt that spans more than 15,000 square feet. Revamped by ELLE Decor A-List designer Miles Redd, the seven-level landmark pays homage to its elegant roots, with preserved plaster moldings, custom millwork, elegant wallcoverings, and heated oak floors in a retro chevron pattern. In the back, a landscaped private garden is paved in limestone and dotted with linden trees that turn golden in the fall. Atop the five-bedroom townhouse, which is listed with Nick Gavin and Amber Gregory of Compass, is a rooftop garden with views of Saint Peter’s Church.

Also inside are a trio of staircases, an elevator servicing six of the seven floors, and two rooftop terraces. #6865. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#6863. Bella Hadid Buys Rustic Colorado Retreat for $5.5 Million

The Victoria’s Secret angel recently started dating someone with strong family ties to the area.

The rugged allure of the Mountain West continues to attract celebrities. Tom Cruise and Oprah Winfrey both own properties in the ski mecca of Telluride. Kevin Costner glamorized multigenerational homesteading with his hit show Yellowstone and, in real life, oversees Aspen’s sprawling Dunbar Ranch. Now, model Bella Hadid—who recently opened the 2026 Victoria’s Secret runway show—just acquired an alpine retreat of her own in a remote town called Crested Butte.

Nestled deep in Colorado’s Rocky Mountains, a roughly three-hour drive from Aspen, Crested Butte has been known as the “Wildflower Capital of Colorado” since 1990, when the state legislature bestowed the honorary title on the Gunnison County town (population: 1,639).

Hadid has reportedly been living in the tiny village for “several months,” per The Wall Street Journal, which was the first to report the sale. The news also comes just days after the model went public on Instagram with new boyfriend Nathan Lacy, a construction manager who has ties to the region.

“I’ve always loved hockey living in NYC,” she wrote, alongside several snaps of her and Lacy at a Colorado Avalanche game. “And being a Colorado girl at heart, growing up here since i was young, I guess it was only a matter of time before I found my way to the @coloradoavalanche.”

Her new creekside compound hit the market over the summer for nearly $7 million with Channing Boucher of LIV Sotheby’s International Realty. Hadid reportedly got it at a discount, paying $5.5 million.

Built in 2001, the property sits on a 0.2-acre parcel with 200 feet of frontage on Coal Creek. Wrapped in weathered lumber, the three-bedroom, three-bathroom retreat was partially constructed out of two “original 100-year-old coal sheds,” per the listing. The rustic interiors, meanwhile, come with 30-foot beamed ceilings and hardwood floors reclaimed from a Virginia barn.

Rounding out the estate is a creek-front dining terrace plus a hot tub at the edge of the water.

This appears to be Hadid’s first real estate transaction since 2021, when she offloaded her New York City penthouse for $6.35 million.

#6860. What’s Your Vacation Vibe?

Whether brand new or reimagined, these four hotels offer distinctive stays. The only question is, which style to choose?

To be a discerning traveler is to have, in equal measure, preference and knowledge of the endlessly evolving hotel landscape. But even the greatest of globetrotters need a guide to what’s new every once in a while. Below, find four recently opened (or reopened) hotels, organized by travel preference: Looking for something in the city? We’ve got you. Country? We have that too. A place to hot air balloon? No worries. The toughest part will just be figuring out where to go first.

Time and Place: Perched atop the Spanish Steps, this 133-year-old mainstay is Old World luxe translated with fresh verve following a lavish update with revamped public spaces by ELLE Decor A-List designer Jacques Garcia.

Design Vibes: Delving into the archives for the Palm Court Garden redo, Garcia collaborated with Italian firm Colleoni Roberto & C. on a green-and-white-striped upholstery and with a Roman blacksmith on vintage-style lanterns.

Hide Out In… One of the damask-covered suites or at the renovated Michelin-starred Imàgo restaurant.

Time and Place: Some will recognize this property as the former home of the Standard, sans human fish tank at the check-in desk. Actually, sans any check-in desk. There’s also no room service, but there is a rooftop park with a gym, a neon blue pool, and 360° views.

Design Vibes: Legendary hotelier Ian Schrager, with his “luxury for all” philosophy, teamed up with minimalist maestro John Pawson on the architecture. Rooms feature custom-made plush seating, pale woods, and custom lighting systems for shifting moods.

Hide Out In… Your own private cineplex. Each room has a projector and 66-inch screen. Or disappear on the dance floor of the Studio, a speakeasy-style nightclub clad in marble and velvet.

Time and Place: Occupying a colonial-style former school in Old Bennington dating from 1829, this intimate inn was brought to life by Rafe Churchill, co-founder of the Elle Decor A-List firm Hendricks Churchill, and Casey Sunderland, Churchill’s partner in the new hospitality firm Place in Mind.

Design Vibes: The hotel leans into its links to a family of writers and to nearby Bennington College with an inviting library stocked with books by alums. Each of the eight unique guestrooms is cozily layered, mixing rich jewel tones and florals with antiques and art that ranges from the Hudson River School to Keith Haring.

Hide Out In… The gardens. Landscape designer Grace Fuller Marroquin lent her wild, cinematic touch to the grounds, which feature an array of native plants as well as a fruit orchard.

Time and Place: Steps from slopes that hosted ski events for two Olympics (1956 and 2026), the hotel occupies a former palazzo from the 1920s.

Design Vibes: Eschewing the traditional Alpine look, designer Achille Salvagni tailored the guestrooms with bespoke references to the local landscape and culture, like plaster bas-reliefs of mountains, ceiling motifs based on traditional clothing embroidery, and hardware in the shape of native wildlife.

Hide Out In… A hot air balloon. The hotel arranges private tours in the off season for unparalleled mountain views. Or, more down to earth, the spa includes a cryo chamber and a plunge pool.

#6858. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6855. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

#6854. Warren Beatty and Annette Bening’s Former L.A. Mansion Lists for Nearly $15 Million

The Hollywood power couple custom-built the gated Beverly Hills house.

The longtime Beverly Hills home of Warren Beatty and Annette Bening will soon hit the market, ELLE Decor can exclusively reveal. The married couple custom-built the gated residence in 1992 and raised their four kids there. The stars are one of Hollywood’s most famous power couples: Beatty has received 14 Academy Award nominations, winning for Best Director in 1982, and three Golden Globe Awards, while Bening has received five Oscar nominations, including Best Actress for 1999’s American Beauty, for which she won the Golden Globe.

The A-list actors parted with their longtime property in 2012, selling it for $6.7 million, per Zillow. Its new asking price will be $14.995 million.

The nine-bedroom estate’s current owners—hotelier Jonathan Baker and his wife, Jenny—acquired the property directly from Beatty and Bening, then spearheaded a $5 million renovation that concluded in 2019. They modified the Mediterranean-style home to have a more contemporary feel, with minimal moldings, glossy surfaces, modern furnishings, and bold splashes of color.

However, they still paid homage to the estate’s movie-star pedigree, creating what they call the “Warren Beatty Theater,” a 17-seat screening room in what was once Beatty’s home office.

Other nods to the legendary actor—who starred in classics like Bonnie and Clyde, Dick Tracy, and Bulworth—included integrating his drop-down projection screen into the new living room. Additional perks include a wine room with a suspended-wire storage system, a wood-paneled dressing room with plenty of built-ins, and a network of terraces.

The home’s amenities continue on the manicured grounds, which feature an infinity-edge pool, a lighted tennis court, multiple al fresco entertaining areas, and a separate guest house.

The couple are parents to four children, among them Ella Beatty, who is following in her parents’ acting footsteps and recently played Lizzie Borden in the Netflix series Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story.

The Beverly Hills house will be listed for $14.995 million with Cory Weiss of Douglas Elliman and Jacob Greene of the Altman Brothers at Douglas Elliman.

#6850. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

Research released around the recent Black Hat USA 2026 conference demonstrates that artificial intelligence is increasing the speed and scale of cybersecurity activity without replacing the attack methods defenders already face.

#6848. How Tiffany & Co. Made a Bird on a Rock Box

Can inspiration be infinite? Yes, if you know where to look.

Above: Tiffany & Co. Singing Bird on a Clock and Bird on a Rock brooch, 800-843-3269.

How does a yellow cockatoo flitting near an open window in Guadeloupe end up a jewelry icon? That bird knew what it was doing when it perched outside the home of Jean Schlumberger, one of Tiffany & Co’s best-known jewelry designers. Nature was always the apple of Schlumberger’s eye, and in his vision that bird would become a diamond-studded feathered friend nestled in lapis lazuli and pinned on Bunny Mellon’s beret (he would go on to make for her a full, whimsical bestiary. Theirs was a partnership of hundreds of jelly fish, camels, and seahorses, plus the beloved fruit and flowers inspired by Mellon’s own garden).

That was 1965. How to keep reaching new heights? A partnership between Tiffany & Co. and the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) in Paris offers inspiration: An archive of thousands of Schlumberger’s sketches, now being restored and digitized, reveal a mind constantly at work and a vision without boundaries. And so it continues, in spessartine and chrysoprase, in rubellites and mother-of-pearl, and turquoise. The next reincarnation of the bird, though, might be one of the most exciting horological spectacles of the year: a glimmering, singing automata atop a burst of sun, a singing gold and titanium bird on a clock. Though, we must note, it does not play music: instead, it tweets. Look, and listen.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6847. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.
